STEP 27 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
So traditional LIMIT is indented-style (body on next line). But in pipe syntax, requirement explicitly says LIMIT is one-line: `|> LIMIT 10` all on
same line. This is a pipe-specific formatting behavior, not global. So we can't just use global `onelineClauses` with 'LIMIT' because that would
change traditional LIMIT formatting (breaking the `supportsLimiting` tests).

Similarly, JOIN in pipe syntax: `|> JOIN ...` should keep content on same line. In traditional queries, JOIN is already formatted as one-line: `JOIN
table ON ...` (formatJoin adds keyword + space, then children). JOIN is not a ClauseNode in traditional query—it's a KeywordNode inside expression,
formatted by `formatJoin`. So if we build a pipe ClauseNode with name 'JOIN', and add 'JOIN' to pipe-specific oneline clauses, that's fine.

So the formatter for pipe clauses needs its own oneline-clause list, separate from the traditional `dialectCfg.onelineClauses`. The requirement says:

"Clauses that the existing formatter treats as indented clauses (`WHERE`, `SELECT`, `ORDER BY`, `AGGREGATE`, `EXTEND`, `SET`, `DROP`) place their body
on a new indented line after the keyword. Clauses that the existing formatter treats as one-line clauses (`LIMIT`, `JOIN` and its variants, `AS`) keep
their content on the same line as the keyword."

So we need a pipe-specific classification:
- Indented: WHERE, SELECT, ORDER BY, AGGREGATE, EXTEND, SET, DROP
- One-line: LIMIT, JOIN variants, AS

Interesting: `SET` and `DROP` are treated as indented in pipe context, even though in traditional BigQuery `SET` (DML UPDATE) is also indented (it's
in reservedClauses and not oneline). `AS` is traditionally a keyword inside expressions (e.g. SELECT ... AS x) formatted inline; in pipe context, it's
a one-line pipe clause `|> AS alias`.

Now, what about the starting `FROM`? It's a traditional indented clause (already works). The first line `FROM` then body on next indented line. The
pipe steps each start at base indentation.

Let me now design the AST and formatter.

#### AST nodes

Add:
```ts
NodeType.pipe = 'pipe',
```

Interfaces:
```ts
export interface PipeNode {
  type: NodeType.pipe;
  pipeOp: KeywordNode; // the "|>" operator? or just pipe node
  clauses: ...
}
```

Actually, let me think about how the grammar can parse. The cleanest approach given the existing grammar's `clause:*` repetition:

Add a `pipe_clause` rule that returns a `PipeNode` containing:
- `pipeOp`: KeywordNode for `|>` (tokenType PIPE, text `|>`)
- `clause`: ClauseNode (for indented/oneline clauses) OR maybe `limit_clause` / `other_clause` / `select_clause` structure.

But the requirement says "Pipe clauses produce structured parse nodes with AGGREGATE and EXTEND promoted to reserved clauses after `|>`". Hmm "produce
structured parse nodes" might mean the AST has distinct pipe nodes rather than treating `|>` as a free-form operator.

Let me consider upstream's actual implementation. I recall that in newer sql-formatter, there's a `PipeNode` with `type: 'pipe'` and fields:
```
{
  type: 'pipe',
  pipe: KeywordNode,
  clauses: [ClauseNode...]
}
```

And in the grammar:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ clause:+
```

Wait, why would a single `|>` be followed by multiple clauses? Because in BigQuery pipe syntax, `|> AGGREGATE ... GROUP BY ...` — GROUP BY is a
sub-clause of AGGREGATE, not a separate pipe step. So the pipe step contains `AGGREGATE ... GROUP BY ...` as one ClauseNode with children that include
a nested `GROUP BY` clause node.

Actually, let me think about grammar. The pipe step is `|> AGGREGATE expr GROUP BY ...`. The `AGGREGATE` is a RESERVED_CLAUSE, then children
expressions, then `GROUP BY` is also a RESERVED_CLAUSE. If we parse it as `other_clause -> %RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*`, then children include
the `GROUP BY` keyword as a free_form_sql keyword (RESERVED_CLAUSE keyword is not part of `keyword` rule — `keyword` only matches RESERVED_KEYWORD,
RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE, RESERVED_JOIN). Wait, is RESERVED_CLAUSE allowed in free_form_sql? Let's check: `free_form_sql` -> asteriskless_free_form_sql
-> ... -> atomic_expression -> keyword -> only RESERVED_KEYWORD, RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE, RESERVED_JOIN. RESERVED_CLAUSE is not included in `keyword`,
so it cannot appear inside free_form_sql! That's why the grammar uses `clause:*` to consume RESERVED_CLAUSE tokens as separate clauses.

So in a pipe step `|> AGGREGATE SUM(x) AS total GROUP BY y`, tokens: PIPE, AGGREGATE(RESERVED_CLAUSE), SUM(x)... AS(RESERVED_KEYWORD), total, GROUP
BY(RESERVED_CLAUSE), y. To parse this, the grammar after PIPE needs to match a sequence: first RESERVED_CLAUSE (AGGREGATE) then free_form_sql then a
nested GROUP BY clause.

If the grammar has `pipe -> %PIPE _ clause:*` where clause matches `other_clause` for AGGREGATE and another `other_clause` for GROUP BY, then we'd get
two ClauseNodes inside the pipe. Then the formatter can format: `|> AGGREGATE` with indented children, and within it, GROUP BY gets another indent
level.

Actually this matches the requirement: "Pipe clauses produce structured parse nodes" and "GROUP BY within AGGREGATE nests as a sub-clause with its own
indentation." So a pipe node contains multiple clauses (the main clause + nested clauses). Let me explore this design.

So AST:
```ts
export interface PipeNode {
  type: NodeType.pipe;
  pipeOp: KeywordNode;  // "|>"
  clauses: ClauseNode[]; // one or more
}
```

Wait, but then how does the formatter know to treat `GROUP BY` as nested inside AGGREGATE vs. as a sibling clause? The requirement says `GROUP BY`
within `AGGREGATE` nests as a sub-clause with its own indentation. If pipe node has `clauses: [AGGREGATE clause, GROUP BY clause]`, the formatter can
format the first clause's children indented, then when it encounters the GROUP BY clause, indent one level deeper than the AGGREGATE keyword. That
means formatPipe should:
1. Add `|> ` and keyword of first clause.
2. If first clause is indented, newline, indent (top level +1), format children.
3. For subsequent clauses in the same pipe (like GROUP BY), format them with an additional indentation level relative to the first clause's children.

Let me define formatter logic:
```
formatPipe(node):
  // node.clauses[0] is main clause
  // pipeOp + first clause name on same line
  // body indented
  // subsequent clauses (GROUP BY) indented one more level
```

But wait, what about `|> AGGREGATE ... GROUP BY ...` where the AGGREGATE body and GROUP BY are both "children" at different levels? Let's write
expected output. BigQuery pipe syntax formatting from Google's docs:

Hmm no. Let's think about the indentation pattern:
- Pipe step line at base indentation: `|> AGGREGATE` (pipe + keyword same line).
- Clause body starts next line, indented one level deeper. So `SUM(x) AS total` at +1.
- GROUP BY sub-clause requires its own indentation level. So `GROUP BY` at +2? "requiring its own indentation level" suggests GROUP BY itself is a
sub-clause with its own indent.

Actually let me look at upstream expected outputs. I recall the sql-formatter test for pipe syntax (from the actual repo). Let me try hard to recall.

Hmm, I'm not sure of exact indentation. Let me think from the issue statement: "The pipe operator and clause keyword share the same line. The clause

- `|> WHERE` on same line.

Let's look at how traditional formatter handles nested clauses. In traditional queries, `SELECT ... GROUP BY ...`:
```
SELECT
  a
FROM
  t
GROUP BY
  a
```
GROUP BY is at top level (base indentation), its body at +1.

In a pipe, the AGGREGATE is at base indentation line `|> AGGREGATE`. Its body is at +1. A nested GROUP BY within AGGREGATE should probably be at +1
(same as AGGREGATE body) with body at +2. That matches "nests as a sub-clause with its own indentation level": GROUP BY itself is indented relative to
the `|>`, and its body indented relative to it.

Actually let's look at the real BigQuery docs formatting. From Google's pipe syntax examples:


I recall Google docs format it as:

```
  ...
|> AGGREGATE
    SUM(y) AS total

```

Hmm. Let me think about what "same indentation pattern the formatter already uses for that clause type" implies. For an indented clause, the formatter
does:
```

  body
```
where WHERE at base indentation and body at +1. In a pipe, `|> WHERE` is at base, and body at +1. For AGGREGATE with GROUP BY, GROUP BY is itself an
indented clause nested inside AGGREGATE. If we format the AGGREGATE clause children as a sub-expression with +1 indentation, and then GROUP BY is a
clause inside those children, the GROUP BY would be formatted at +1 with body at +2. That seems natural.

Now what about `|> AS foo`? One-line: `|> AS foo` on same line. And `|> JOIN table ON ...` one-line. And `|> LIMIT 10` one-line.

What about pipe query starting FROM nested in parentheses? The grammar already allows clauses inside parentheses via `parenthesis -> "("
expressions_or_clauses ")"`, and pipe clauses would be part of expressions_or_clauses, so nesting works.

Let me now design grammar.

#### Grammar design

Add to `clause`:
```
| pipe
```

`pipe`:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ pipe_clause:+ {% ... %}
```

where `pipe_clause` matches the clause after `|>`.

But we need to handle comments between `|>` and the keyword. And we need the keyword to be one of the allowed token types. Let me define:

```
pipe_clause ->
  ( %RESERVED_CLAUSE
  | %RESERVED_SELECT
  | %RESERVED_JOIN
  | %LIMIT
  | %RESERVED_KEYWORD (with text AS?)
  ) ...
```

Hmm, the grammar `%RESERVED_KEYWORD` would match ANY reserved keyword, which includes things like `FROM`, `WHERE`? Wait, `FROM` and `WHERE` are in
`reservedClauses`, so they're RESERVED_CLAUSE, not RESERVED_KEYWORD. `AS` is in `keywords` (reservedKeywords), so it's RESERVED_KEYWORD. But also
`SELECT` is RESERVED_SELECT. `ORDER BY` is RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE? Let's check: in tokenizer, RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE is matched before
RESERVED_CLAUSE? Actually buildRulesBeforeParams order: RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE, RESERVED_DATA_TYPE_PHRASE, CASE, END, BETWEEN, LIMIT,
RESERVED_CLAUSE, RESERVED_SELECT, ... So `ORDER BY` would be RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE (it's in reservedKeywordPhrases? No! `ORDER BY` is in
reservedClauses, not reservedKeywordPhrases). Let me check tokenizer rule ordering: RESERVED_KEYWORD_PHRASE matches cfg.reservedKeywordPhrases (which
doesn't include ORDER BY). Then RESERVED_CLAUSE matches cfg.reservedClauses including 'ORDER BY'. So ORDER BY is RESERVED_CLAUSE. Good.

`GROUP BY` is RESERVED_CLAUSE too.

So pipe_clause keyword can be:
- RESERVED_CLAUSE (WHERE, ORDER BY, AGGREGATE, EXTEND, SET, DROP, GROUP BY, etc.)
- RESERVED_SELECT (SELECT)
- RESERVED_JOIN (JOIN variants)
- LIMIT (LIMIT)
- RESERVED_KEYWORD (AS)

For the pipe clause grammar, we need to parse children. For most clauses, children are free_form_sql tokens until the next clause/pipe/EOF. But the
grammar is context-free; we can write:

```
pipe_clause -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*
pipe_clause -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_SELECT ...
pipe_clause -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_JOIN ...
pipe_clause -> %PIPE _ %LIMIT ...
pipe_clause -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_KEYWORD ...
```

But this creates ambiguity because `free_form_sql:*` greedily consumes following clauses? In Nearley, `*` is not greedy; it's a CFG. The grammar would
be ambiguous because `|> AGGREGATE foo GROUP BY bar` could parse as:
1. `%RESERVED_CLAUSE` AGGREGATE with children [foo] followed by another clause GROUP BY...
2. `%RESERVED_CLAUSE` AGGREGATE with children [foo, GROUP BY keyword?, bar] — but RESERVED_CLAUSE can't be in free_form_sql, so option 2 isn't
possible.

Actually, RESERVED_CLAUSE tokens cannot be consumed by free_form_sql, so the grammar naturally splits at each RESERVED_CLAUSE. That's how the existing
grammar works: `expressions_or_clauses -> free_form_sql:* clause:*`, where `clause` matches RESERVED_CLAUSE etc.

So if we write:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ clause:+
```
where `clause` includes other_clause (RESERVED_CLAUSE), select_clause (RESERVED_SELECT), limit_clause, and we add join/AS handling. Then `|> AGGREGATE
foo GROUP BY bar` parses as pipe with clauses [AGGREGATE clause, GROUP BY clause].

But there's an issue: `select_clause` has special grammar (requires all_columns_asterisk or asteriskless_free_form_sql then free_form_sql:*). That's
fine. `limit_clause` has special grammar. `other_clause` handles RESERVED_CLAUSE with free_form_sql:*. We need to handle RESERVED_JOIN and
RESERVED_KEYWORD (AS) as clauses too.

But wait, `clause` is also used in traditional queries. If we add `pipe` to `clause`, that's fine. But the existing `clause` alternatives
(select_clause, other_clause, etc.) already parse the keyword after `|>`. The `pipe` rule just needs `%PIPE _ clause:+`.

Let me define:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ pipe_clauses {% ... %}
pipe_clauses -> clause:+
```

Hmm but then each clause in the pipe is a standard ClauseNode. The `pipe` node wraps them. For JOIN and AS, we need a new clause type. Let's add:

```
other_clause -> %RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*  // existing
join_clause -> %RESERVED_JOIN free_form_sql:*  // new
keyword_clause -> %RESERVED_KEYWORD free_form_sql:*  // new, for AS
```

Wait but AS is in reservedKeywords. If we make a generic `keyword_clause` matching any RESERVED_KEYWORD, then in traditional queries `AS` inside
SELECT would be parsed as a clause instead of a keyword inside expression, breaking things. We only want AS as a clause after `|>`. So we need to
restrict pipe_clause's keyword handling to pipe context.

So pipe_clause should be separate from traditional `clause`:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ pipe_clause:+
pipe_clause ->
  ( %RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*
  | %RESERVED_SELECT ...
  | %RESERVED_JOIN ...
  | %LIMIT ...
  | %RESERVED_KEYWORD ... )  // AS only
```

But we still have ambiguity issue for GROUP BY: `%RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*` followed by another `%RESERVED_CLAUSE` — since RESERVED_CLAUSE
isn't in free_form_sql, the split is deterministic. Good.

But there's a subtle issue: `free_form_sql:*` can consume zero or more. If it consumes zero, then `|> WHERE` followed by EOF is valid. Fine.

Let me design the AST for pipe_clause:
- For RESERVED_CLAUSE: ClauseNode (like other_clause)
- For RESERVED_SELECT: ClauseNode (like select_clause)
- For RESERVED_JOIN: ClauseNode
- For LIMIT: ClauseNode (or LimitClauseNode?)
- For RESERVED_KEYWORD (AS): ClauseNode

The formatter needs to distinguish one-line vs indented for pipe. It can use the clause name text and a pipe-specific oneline set.

Let me define a `PipeNode`:
```ts
export interface PipeNode {
  type: NodeType.pipe;
  pipeOp: KeywordNode;  // text '|>', tokenType PIPE
  clauses: ClauseNode[];  // one or more
}
```

Now formatter `formatPipe`:
```
private formatPipe(node: PipeNode) {
  // format pipeOp
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.pipeOp), WS.SPACE);
  // format first clause
  ...
}
```

Wait — but the `pipe` node appears in the statement children list alongside other clauses. For a query `FROM table |> WHERE ...`, the children are
[FROM clause, table expr, pipe node]. The ExpressionFormatter formats nodes in order. For the FROM clause, `formatClause` adds `WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT`
before FROM and body indented. Then the pipe node should be formatted starting with `WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT` then `|> WHERE ...`. Since the indentation
state after FROM's body is back to base (formatClauseInIndentedStyle decreases after), the pipe's `WS.INDENT` will be at base. Good.

Now formatting each pipe clause:
```
formatPipe(node):
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.pipeOp), WS.SPACE);
  const [first, ...rest] = node.clauses;
  this.formatPipeClause(first, 1); // at +1 level
  for (const clause of rest) {
    // nested sub-clause: format with additional indentation
  }
```

But the pipeClause formatting itself needs to handle both one-line and indented:
- For indented pipe clause (WHERE, SELECT, ORDER BY, AGGREGATE, EXTEND, SET, DROP):
  ```
  |> WHERE
      body
  ```
  So after `|> ` + keyword, newline, indent (+1), body.
- For one-line pipe clause (LIMIT, JOIN, AS):
  ```
  |> LIMIT 10
  ```
  After `|> ` + keyword + space, body on same line.

For GROUP BY nested within AGGREGATE: if the pipe node has clauses [AGGREGATE, GROUP BY], we need:
```
|> AGGREGATE
    SUM(a) AS total
    GROUP BY
      b
```
Wait, the GROUP BY is at +1 (same as AGGREGATE body) with its own body at +2. So when formatting rest clauses, each rest clause is an indented clause
at the level of the first clause's body (which is +1 relative to pipe), and its own body at +2.

But what if a pipe has `|> WHERE x |> SELECT y`? That's two separate pipe nodes, not one pipe with two clauses. Because each `|>` starts a new pipe
node. So a single pipe node's clauses list is the sequence of clauses after one `|>`. For `|> AGGREGATE ... GROUP BY ...`, both AGGREGATE and GROUP BY
are after the same `|>`, so one pipe node with clauses [AGGREGATE, GROUP BY].

Now, how does the grammar know to stop consuming clauses after `|>`? For `|> WHERE a > 1 |> SELECT ...`, after the PIPE, the clause parser sees WHERE,
then free_form_sql `a > 1`, then PIPE token. PIPE is not a RESERVED_CLAUSE, so `pipe_clause:+` stops at PIPE. Good. Then the outer `clause:*` in
expressions_or_clauses will parse the next PIPE as another pipe node.

Now, there's a subtlety: the existing `expressions_or_clauses -> free_form_sql:* clause:*` requires all free_form_sql before clauses. For pipe query
`FROM table |> WHERE ...`, after FROM clause, `table` is free_form_sql, then pipe node is a clause. That works: expressions_or_clauses =
[free_form_sql: [], clause: [FROM, pipe, pipe...]]. Actually the FROM clause is parsed as a clause, then table as... wait order: `free_form_sql:*
clause:*`. If free_form_sql:* consumes table first, then clause:* consumes FROM, pipe, etc. Yes: table is free_form_sql (before clauses), then FROM
clause, pipe clauses. So children order: [table, FROM clause, pipe, pipe].

Hmm wait, but the existing grammar allows `FROM table WHERE ...` as [free_form_sql: table, clause: FROM, clause: WHERE]. Let me verify with the parser
test. Actually in current formatter output for `FROM table WHERE a > 1`, we got:
```
FROM
  table
WHERE
  a > 1
```
So children order is [FROM clause, table, WHERE clause]? Or [table, FROM, WHERE]? The formatter formats FROM (newline+indent), then table (space),
then WHERE (newline+indent). Either order gives same output because FROM adds newline before it and table is added inline. Let's not worry.

Now, we need to add `pipe` to the `clause` rule. Let me check: `clause` currently is used both in `expressions_or_clauses` and inside `parenthesis`
(via expressions_or_clauses). Adding pipe there covers nesting.

But there's a problem: `clause -> (limit_clause | select_clause | other_clause | set_operation | pipe)`. The pipe rule starts with `%PIPE`, which is
distinct, so no ambiguity with other clauses.

Now, `pipe_clause` parsing. Let me define in grammar:

```
pipe_clause ->
  ( %RESERVED_CLAUSE
  | %RESERVED_SELECT
  | %RESERVED_JOIN
  | %LIMIT
  | %RESERVED_KEYWORD ) _ free_form_sql:*
```

But we need the `_` comments between keyword and body to attach. Actually let's mirror other_clause which does `%RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*` (no
`_`; comments become part of free_form_sql via comment nodes). Hmm, comments between clause keyword and body: `other_clause` doesn't have `_` before
children; comments are matched by free_form_sql (comment is part of asteriskless_free_form_sql). So `WHERE --comment\n x` -> children include comment
node. Fine.

For select, we need the special handling of `*` (all_columns_asterisk). Existing `select_clause` has:
```
select_clause -> %RESERVED_SELECT (all_columns_asterisk free_form_sql:* | asteriskless_free_form_sql free_form_sql:*)
```
We can reuse `select_clause` directly inside pipe_clause. Actually if pipe_clause delegates to the existing clause types, that's cleaner:

```
pipe_clause -> ( select_clause | other_clause | join_clause | limit_clause | keyword_clause )
```

But wait, `other_clause` matches any RESERVED_CLAUSE. That's fine. `limit_clause` matches LIMIT and has special offset/count structure.
`select_clause` matches SELECT. For JOIN and AS, we need new clause rules.

Let me define:
```
pipe_clause -> select_clause | other_clause | join_clause | limit_clause | keyword_clause

join_clause -> %RESERVED_JOIN _ free_form_sql:* {% ... %}
keyword_clause -> %RESERVED_KEYWORD _ free_form_sql:* {% ... %}  // for AS
```

But this has a problem: `other_clause` matches RESERVED_CLAUSE including GROUP BY. That's good for AGGREGATE + GROUP BY.

Now the AST for these:
- `join_clause` returns ClauseNode with nameKw tokenType RESERVED_JOIN.
- `keyword_clause` returns ClauseNode with nameKw tokenType RESERVED_KEYWORD.

But careful: keyword_clause matching ANY RESERVED_KEYWORD could cause issues. In pipe context, e.g. `|> AS alias` — AS is RESERVED_KEYWORD. But what
about `|> AGGREGATE ...` — AGGREGATE promoted to RESERVED_CLAUSE, so it's other_clause. Good. Are there other RESERVED_KEYWORD tokens in pipe clauses?
e.g. `|> WHERE a AS b`? The `AS` inside WHERE's body would be parsed as a keyword_clause (because after pipe_clause's free_form_sql, the next clause
could be keyword_clause). Hmm. Example: `|> SELECT a AS b` — SELECT clause children [a], then `AS` (RESERVED_KEYWORD) would be parsed as
keyword_clause (AS clause), then b as... wait after keyword_clause consumes free_form_sql [b]. So the AST would be pipe clauses [SELECT clause
(children [a]), keyword_clause AS (children [b])]. Then formatter would output:
```
|> SELECT
    a
    |> AS b    <- no, this is within same pipe
```
Wait no, keyword_clause is within the same pipe node, so formatPipe would format it as a nested clause at +1:
```
|> SELECT
    a
    AS
      b  (if AS is indented)
```
But requirement says AS is one-line: `AS b`. But more importantly, `SELECT a AS b` should format as `|> SELECT a AS b`? Hmm, in pipe syntax, `AS` is
used to name intermediates, like `|> AS foo`, or in expressions `SELECT a AS b`. If `AS` is in reservedKeywords and keyword_clause matches any
RESERVED_KEYWORD, it would break normal pipe SELECT expressions.

So we should only treat `AS` as a pipe clause when it's actually the pipe clause keyword, i.e., immediately after `|>` (after optional comments). We
should NOT match AS as a clause within the body of another pipe clause.

This means we can't reuse the general `clause` machinery for pipe clauses, because `clause:*` would greedily split at AS inside expressions.

How does the existing grammar handle `SELECT a AS b` in traditional queries? `AS` is RESERVED_KEYWORD, and `keyword` rule in atomic_expression
includes RESERVED_KEYWORD, so `AS` is part of free_form_sql (as a keyword node). So `SELECT a AS b` -> select_clause children [a, AS, b]. Good.

For pipe, if we write `pipe_clause -> select_clause` and then `pipe_clause:+`, after SELECT children [a], the next token AS is RESERVED_KEYWORD. If we
have a `keyword_clause` alternative, the grammar would split. To avoid this, we should NOT include AS in the general `pipe_clause` repeated parsing.
Instead, AS should only be allowed as the *first* pipe clause keyword directly after `|>`.

So the grammar should be:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ pipe_clause:+
```
where `pipe_clause` alternatives are select_clause, other_clause, join_clause, limit_clause — but NOT keyword_clause. And a separate rule for pipe
starting with AS:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_KEYWORD ... (only AS)
```

But then how do we prevent `AS` after `|>` from being consumed as part of a previous pipe clause's free_form_sql? Since `AS` IS part of free_form_sql
(keyword), `|> AS foo` would be parsed as a pipe_clause? Let's trace: after `|>`, we need a pipe_clause. The pipe_clause alternatives start with
RESERVED_CLAUSE/SELECT/JOIN/LIMIT. AS is RESERVED_KEYWORD, so none match; then the dedicated AS pipe rule matches `%PIPE _ %RESERVED_KEYWORD`. Good.
But what about `|> SELECT a AS b`? After `|>`, pipe_clause = select_clause. select_clause's `free_form_sql:*` consumes `a`, `AS`, `b` (AS is a keyword
in free_form_sql). Then at the end, no more pipe_clause because next is EOF. So `pipe_clause:+` stops. Good.

But wait: `free_form_sql:*` in `other_clause` can consume RESERVED_KEYWORD tokens (like AS) but not RESERVED_CLAUSE. So `|> WHERE a AS b` ->
other_clause children [a, AS, b]. Good.

What about `|> AGGREGATE SUM(a) AS total GROUP BY b`? After `|>`, pipe_clause = other_clause AGGREGATE with children [SUM(a), AS, total]. Then next
token GROUP BY is RESERVED_CLAUSE, so pipe_clause:+ continues with other_clause GROUP BY children [b]. Great, this gives pipe node clauses [AGGREGATE,
GROUP BY].

So the design:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ pipe_clause:+ {% ... %}
pipe -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_KEYWORD free_form_sql:* {% ... %}  // AS as first clause
```
Actually if we want AS as first clause and it's the only clause (since after AS, no more clauses), we can make a dedicated `as_pipe` rule:
```
pipe -> %PIPE _ %RESERVED_KEYWORD _ free_form_sql:* {% ... %}
```
But this would also match `|> AS foo AS bar`? After AS children [foo], then AS is again RESERVED_KEYWORD; but free_form_sql:* would consume it too
(keyword), so children [foo, AS, bar]. That's fine—AS is a keyword in expressions too.

Hmm, but should we restrict to only AS keyword? In BigQuery pipe, only AS among RESERVED_KEYWORD can start a pipe clause. Other keywords like `SAFE`,
`ALL`, etc. shouldn't. But if we match any RESERVED_KEYWORD as a pipe clause, then `|> SAFE ...` would be parsed as a pipe. That's probably harmless
but not ideal. Better to restrict to AS.

How to restrict in Nearley? We can check `token.text === 'AS'` in the postprocessor. The rule `%RESERVED_KEYWORD` matches any reserved keyword; in the
postprocessor, if text isn't AS, return a failure? Nearley doesn't support failure in postprocessor easily. Alternative: put AS in a separate
tokenizer category? Not worth it. We can add a token type for AS? That's invasive.

Actually maybe simpler: don't make a dedicated AS rule. Instead, make `pipe_clause` include `keyword_clause` but only allow RESERVED_KEYWORD at the
*start* of the pipe (first pipe_clause), not in the repetition. Let me structure:

```
pipe -> %PIPE _ first_pipe_clause rest_pipe_clause:*
first_pipe_clause -> ( select_clause | other_clause | join_clause | limit_clause | keyword_clause )
rest_pipe_clause -> ( select_clause | other_clause | join_clause | limit_clause )
```

But `keyword_clause` matches any RESERVED_KEYWORD. For `|> AS foo`, first_pipe_clause = keyword_clause AS children [foo]. For `|> SELECT a AS b`,
first_pipe_clause = select_clause (since select_clause alternative comes before keyword_clause? In Nearley, alternatives are tried; select_clause
starts with RESERVED_SELECT, so it matches SELECT, and its free_form_sql:* consumes AS b, so keyword_clause alternative never reached because the
first alternative succeeds). For `|> WHERE a AS b`, other_clause matches WHERE and consumes AS b. For `|> SAFE ...`, keyword_clause matches SAFE as a
pipe clause. That would create a weird pipe but it's not a realistic query.

Actually wait, there's a bigger issue: the `rest_pipe_clause` repetition. For `|> AGGREGATE SUM(a) AS total GROUP BY b`, rest_pipe_clause matches
GROUP BY (other_clause). Good. For `|> SELECT a AS b`, after select_clause consumes everything, rest_pipe_clause:* sees no more tokens. Good.

But for `|> WHERE a AS b`, other_clause consumes `a AS b` as children, so no split. Good.

What about `|> AS foo GROUP BY bar`? Is that valid pipe syntax? AS names an intermediate, then... probably you can't have GROUP BY after AS without
another `|>`. But grammar would parse first_pipe_clause=keyword_clause AS children [foo], then rest_pipe_clause matches GROUP BY. Then formatter would
format AS (one-line) then GROUP BY nested. Not realistic but harmless.

Now, there's still a problem with `keyword_clause` matching any RESERVED_KEYWORD as first pipe clause. Could break e.g. `|> AGGREGATE ...`? AGGREGATE
is RESERVED_CLAUSE (promoted), not keyword. `|> EXTEND ...` likewise. `|> SET ...`, `DROP ...` are RESERVED_CLAUSE. `|> AS` is the only realistic
RESERVED_KEYWORD. But what about `|> WHERE ...`? WHERE is RESERVED_CLAUSE, not keyword. Good.

However, there's a subtle grammar ambiguity: `pipe -> %PIPE _ first_pipe_clause rest_pipe_clause:*` with keyword_clause as an alternative for
first_pipe_clause. For input `|> SELECT a AS b`, both:
- first_pipe_clause = select_clause (consumes SELECT a AS b), rest = []
- first_pipe_clause = select_clause? No, keyword_clause can't match SELECT.
No ambiguity.

For `|> WHERE a`, both other_clause and ... no, keyword_clause can't match WHERE. Fine.

For `|> AS x`, only keyword_clause matches. Fine.

Now, the postprocessor for pipe node: We need to combine first_pipe_clause + rest_pipe_clause into a PipeNode with `clauses` array.

Let me define AST:
```ts
export interface PipeNode {
  type: NodeType.pipe;
  pipeOp: KeywordNode;
  clauses: ClauseNode[];
}
```

And formatter.

#### Tokenizer: PIPE rule

Add `PIPE = 'PIPE'` to TokenType enum. Add to isReserved? Not needed; PIPE is not a reserved word.

Add tokenizer rule:
```
{ type: TokenType.PIPE, regex: /[|]>/y },
```
placed in `buildRulesAfterParams` before OPERATOR. Actually, where should PIPE go? The `|>` is a symbol, so it belongs in afterParams with operators.
Put it before OPERATOR.

But wait, there's also `||` operator. `|>` and `||` both start with `|`. Since regex is `/[|]>/y` with sticky flag, it matches exactly at current
position. Order matters: PIPE before OPERATOR ensures `|>` matches as PIPE; if OPERATOR came first and matched `|`, then `>` would be separate. So
PIPE first.

#### BigQuery formatter changes

1. Add pipe-exclusive clauses to reservedClauses: `AGGREGATE`, `EXTEND`, `SET`, `DROP`, `AS`. Wait, SET and DROP are already in reservedClauses (SET
for DML; DROP for DROP COLUMN etc.). AS is not in reservedClauses. AGGREGATE and EXTEND are not.

But requirement says "AGGREGATE and EXTEND promoted to reserved clauses after `|>`". So we should NOT put AGGREGATE/EXTEND in reservedClauses globally
(otherwise they'd be RESERVED_CLAUSE everywhere, which could change traditional queries where AGGREGATE/EXTEND might be identifiers or functions).
Instead, tokenizer postProcess should promote them after PIPE.

Let's design postProcess:
```
function postProcess(tokens: Token[]): Token[] {
  return promotePipeClauseKeywords(detectArraySubscripts(combineParameterizedTypes(tokens)));
}
```

`promotePipeClauseKeywords`:
```
function promotePipeClauseKeywords(tokens: Token[]) {
  let prevToken = EOF_TOKEN;
  return tokens.map(token => {
    if (prevToken.type === TokenType.PIPE && ['AGGREGATE', 'EXTEND'].includes(token.text)) {
      prevToken = token;
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.RESERVED_CLAUSE };
    }
    prevToken = token;
    return token;
  });
}
```

Wait, but requirement says "AGGREGATE and EXTEND promoted to reserved clauses after `|>`." What about SET, DROP, AS? SET and DROP are already
RESERVED_CLAUSE globally. AS is RESERVED_KEYWORD globally (needed for expressions). So only AGGREGATE and EXTEND need promotion. And AS remains
RESERVED_KEYWORD and is handled specially in grammar.

But also, what about `GROUP BY` within AGGREGATE? GROUP BY is already RESERVED_CLAUSE globally. Good.

2. Add to `reservedClauses`? We don't add AGGREGATE/EXTEND. AS not added. So only postProcess changes.

Actually wait, there's a subtlety with `postProcess` order. Current postProcess is `detectArraySubscripts(combineParameterizedTypes(tokens))`. We'll
add promotion.

But there's a problem: AGGREGATE/EXTEND might appear as identifiers in traditional queries (e.g., a column named `aggregate`). If they're only
promoted after PIPE, that's safe.

3. `keywordCase` governs pipe keywords including `|>`? The `|>` operator doesn't have case. The pipe-exclusive keywords AGGREGATE, EXTEND, SET, DROP,
AS are KeywordNodes, so showKw handles keywordCase. Good.

about the cleanest implementation. Since the formatter's `formatPipe` can just hardcode the list based on clause text, or use a dialect config. Better

But JOIN variants as single clause names: the ClauseNode's nameKw.text for `LEFT JOIN` would be 'LEFT JOIN' (RESERVED_JOIN token text). Actually

Alternatively, simpler: hardcode a set in ExpressionFormatter for pipe clauses. But data-driven is cleaner and matches the existing design.

import { last } from '../utils.js';

import Indentation from './Indentation.js';

/** Whitespace modifiers to be used with add() method */
export enum WS {
  SPACE, // Adds single space
  NO_SPACE, // Removes preceding horizontal whitespace (if any)
  NO_NEWLINE, // Removes all preceding whitespace (whether horizontal or vertical)
  NEWLINE, // Adds single newline (and removes any preceding whitespace)
  MANDATORY_NEWLINE, // Adds single newline that can't be removed by NO_NEWLINE
  INDENT, // Adds indentation (as much as needed for current indentation level)
  SINGLE_INDENT, // Adds whitespace for single indentation step
}

export type LayoutItem = WS.SPACE | WS.SINGLE_INDENT | WS.NEWLINE | WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE | string;

/**
 * API for constructing SQL string (especially the whitespace part).
 *
 * It hides the internal implementation.
 * Originally it used plain string concatenation, which was expensive.
 * Now it's storing items to array and builds the string only in the end.
 */
export default class Layout {
  private items: LayoutItem[] = [];

  constructor(public indentation: Indentation) {}

  /**
   * Appends token strings and whitespace modifications to SQL string.
   */
  public add(...items: (WS | string)[]) {
    for (const item of items) {
      switch (item) {
        case WS.SPACE:
          this.items.push(WS.SPACE);
          break;
        case WS.NO_SPACE:
          this.trimHorizontalWhitespace();
          break;
        case WS.NO_NEWLINE:
          this.trimWhitespace();
          break;
        case WS.NEWLINE:
          this.trimHorizontalWhitespace();
          this.addNewline(WS.NEWLINE);
          break;
        case WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE:
          this.trimHorizontalWhitespace();
          this.addNewline(WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE);
          break;
        case WS.INDENT:
          this.addIndentation();
          break;
        case WS.SINGLE_INDENT:
          this.items.push(WS.SINGLE_INDENT);
          break;
        default:
          this.items.push(item);
      }
    }
  }

  private trimHorizontalWhitespace() {
    while (isHorizontalWhitespace(last(this.items))) {
      this.items.pop();
    }
  }

  private trimWhitespace() {
    while (isRemovableWhitespace(last(this.items))) {
      this.items.pop();
    }
  }

  private addNewline(newline: WS.NEWLINE | WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE) {
    if (this.items.length > 0) {
      switch (last(this.items)) {
        case WS.NEWLINE:
          this.items.pop();
          this.items.push(newline);
          break;
        case WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE:
          // keep as is
          break;
        default:
          this.items.push(newline);
          break;
      }
    }
  }

  private addIndentation() {
    for (let i = 0; i < this.indentation.getLevel(); i++) {
      this.items.push(WS.SINGLE_INDENT);
    }
  }

  /**
   * Returns the final SQL string.
   */
  public toString(): string {
    return this.items.map(item => this.itemToString(item)).join('');
  }

  /**
   * Returns the internal layout data
   */
  public getLayoutItems(): LayoutItem[] {
    return this.items;
  }

  private itemToString(item: LayoutItem): string {
    switch (item) {
      case WS.SPACE:
        return ' ';
      case WS.NEWLINE:
      case WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE:
        return '\n';
      case WS.SINGLE_INDENT:
        return this.indentation.getSingleIndent();
      default:
        return item;
    }
  }
}

const isHorizontalWhitespace = (item: WS | string | undefined) =>
  item === WS.SPACE || item === WS.SINGLE_INDENT;

const isRemovableWhitespace = (item: WS | string | undefined) =>
  item === WS.SPACE || item === WS.SINGLE_INDENT || item === WS.NEWLINE;
===INDENT===
import { last } from '../utils.js';

const INDENT_TYPE_TOP_LEVEL = 'top-level';
const INDENT_TYPE_BLOCK_LEVEL = 'block-level';

/**
 * Manages indentation levels.
 *
 * There are two types of indentation levels:
 *
 * - BLOCK_LEVEL : increased by open-parenthesis
 * - TOP_LEVEL : increased by RESERVED_CLAUSE words
 */
export default class Indentation {
  private indentTypes: string[] = [];

  /**
   * @param {string} indent A string to indent with
   */
  constructor(private indent: string) {}

  /**
   * Returns indentation string for single indentation step.
   */
  getSingleIndent(): string {
    return this.indent;
  }

  /**
   * Returns current indentation level
   */
  getLevel(): number {
    return this.indentTypes.length;
  }

  /**
   * Increases indentation by one top-level indent.
   */
  increaseTopLevel() {
    this.indentTypes.push(INDENT_TYPE_TOP_LEVEL);
  }

  /**
   * Increases indentation by one block-level indent.
   */
  increaseBlockLevel() {
    this.indentTypes.push(INDENT_TYPE_BLOCK_LEVEL);
  }

  /**
   * Decreases indentation by one top-level indent.
   * Does nothing when the previous indent is not top-level.
   */
  decreaseTopLevel() {
    if (this.indentTypes.length > 0 && last(this.indentTypes) === INDENT_TYPE_TOP_LEVEL) {
      this.indentTypes.pop();
    }
  }

  /**
   * Decreases indentation by one block-level indent.
   * If there are top-level indents within the block-level indent,
   * throws away these as well.
   */
  decreaseBlockLevel() {
    while (this.indentTypes.length > 0) {
      const type = this.indentTypes.pop();
      if (type !== INDENT_TYPE_TOP_LEVEL) {
        break;
      }
    }
  }
}
===CONFIG===
import { FormatOptions } from '../FormatOptions.js';

// Utility functions for config options

/**
 * Creates a string to use for one step of indentation.
 */
export function indentString(cfg: FormatOptions): string {
  if (cfg.indentStyle === 'tabularLeft' || cfg.indentStyle === 'tabularRight') {
    return ' '.repeat(10);
  }
  if (cfg.useTabs) {
    return '\t';
  }
  return ' '.repeat(cfg.tabWidth);
}

/**
 * True when indentStyle is one of the tabular ones.
 */
export function isTabularStyle(cfg: FormatOptions): boolean {
  return cfg.indentStyle === 'tabularLeft' || cfg.indentStyle === 'tabularRight';
}

Let me trace `formatClauseInIndentedStyle`:
```

this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();
this.layout.add(WS.INDENT);
```

So for `FROM table` at top-level:
- add newline, indent (level 0 -> no indent), "FROM", newline
- increaseTopLevel -> level 1
- add indent -> "  "
- format children "table"
- decreaseTopLevel -> level 0

Result:
```
FROM
  table
```

For a pipe node, we want:
```
|> WHERE
    a > 1
```
where `|>` is at level 0, and `WHERE` body at level 1 (relative to the pipe's indentation context). But in nested parentheses, the base indentation is
level N (block level), and pipe adds on top of that.

Let me think about the pipe formatting:

```
formatPipe(node):
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT);  // newline at current level
  this.layout.add(this.showKw(node.pipeOp), WS.SPACE);  // "|> "
  // format first clause
  ...
```

Actually, let's reuse the existing clause formatting logic but with the pipe prefix. For the first clause:
- If one-line: `|> LIMIT 10`. So:
  ```
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(pipeOp), WS.SPACE, showKw(nameKw), WS.SPACE);
  format children
  ```
- If indented: `|> WHERE\n  body`:
  ```
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(pipeOp), WS.SPACE, showKw(nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
  increaseTopLevel();
  add(WS.INDENT);
  format children;
  decreaseTopLevel();
  ```

For subsequent clauses in same pipe (e.g., GROUP BY nested):
- We want GROUP BY at the same indentation as the first clause's body, with its own body one deeper. So:
  ```

  // for GROUP BY:
  add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
  increaseTopLevel();
  add(WS.INDENT);
  format children;
  decreaseTopLevel();
  ```
  At this point, indentation is N+1 (after first clause body formatting and decrease? Let's trace).

Let's trace formatPipe for `|> AGGREGATE ... GROUP BY ...` at base level 0:
1. add newline, indent(0), "|> ", "AGGREGATE", newline
2. increaseTopLevel -> level 1
3. add indent -> "  "
4. format children "SUM(a) AS total" -> at level 1
5. Now for GROUP BY: add newline, indent(1) -> "  ", "GROUP BY", newline; increaseTopLevel -> level 2; add indent -> "    "; format children "b";
decreaseTopLevel -> level 1.

```
    SUM(a) AS total

      b
```
Wait step 3: at level 1, indent is "  " (2 spaces for tabWidth 2). Step 5: newline, indent(1) "  ", "GROUP BY", newline, then increase to level 2,
indent "    " (4 spaces), "b". So output:
```
|> AGGREGATE
    SUM(a) AS total
    GROUP BY
      b
```
Wait, "  " is 2 spaces, "    " is 4 spaces. Let me count: with 2-space indent:
- `|> AGGREGATE` (0 spaces)
- `  SUM(a) AS total` (2 spaces)
- `  GROUP BY` (2 spaces)
- `    b` (4 spaces)

Yes that matches my expectation: GROUP BY at same level as AGGREGATE body, b one deeper.

But is that the desired output? Let me reconsider. The requirement says "AGGREGATE with an optional nested GROUP BY sub-clause requiring its own
indentation level". "requiring its own indentation level" could mean GROUP BY is at +1 relative to `|>`, and its body at +2. That's what we have. I
think that's right.

But wait, what if the pipe clause is one-line? E.g. `|> AS foo` — subsequent clauses unlikely. For one-line first clause followed by nested clause

Now, how to structure `formatPipe` in code. The pipe node has `clauses: ClauseNode[]`. We format the first clause with the pipe prefix, then remaining
clauses as nested sub-clauses.

Let me write:
```ts
private formatPipe(node: PipeNode) {
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.pipeOp), WS.SPACE);
  const [first, ...rest] = node.clauses;
  this.formatPipeClause(first, /* isFirst: */ true);
  // rest are nested
  for (const clause of rest) {
    this.formatNestedPipeClause(clause);
  }
}
```

But the formatting of a pipe clause depends on whether it's one-line or indented. Let me write helper methods.

Actually, we can reuse existing `formatClauseInOnelineStyle` / `formatClauseInIndentedStyle`, but those add `WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT` before the
keyword. We don't want that for the first clause because pipeOp already added. For subsequent clauses, we do want newline+indent before the keyword.

Let me write:
```ts
private formatPipe(node: PipeNode) {
  const [first, ...rest] = node.clauses;
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.pipeOp), WS.SPACE);

  if (this.isOnelinePipeClause(first)) {
    this.formatPipeClauseInOnelineStyle(first);
  } else {
    this.formatPipeClauseInIndentedStyle(first);
  }

  for (const clause of rest) {
    if (this.isOnelinePipeClause(clause)) {
      this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT);
      this.formatPipeClauseInOnelineStyle(clause);
    } else {
      this.formatNestedPipeClauseInIndentedStyle(clause);
    }
  }
}
```

Where:
- `formatPipeClauseInOnelineStyle(clause)`: `layout.add(showKw(nameKw), WS.SPACE); layout = formatSubExpression(children);` — but wait,
`formatSubExpression` returns a new ExpressionFormatter sharing the same layout. Since we're using `this.layout`, we can call
`this.formatSubExpression(node.children)` which returns a Layout but also mutates this.layout? Let me check `formatSubExpression`:
```
private formatSubExpression(nodes: AstNode[]): Layout {
  return new ExpressionFormatter({... layout: this.layout ...}).format(nodes);
}
```
It creates a new ExpressionFormatter with same layout, and format returns layout. In existing code they do `this.layout =
this.formatSubExpression(...)` to get the potentially new layout (though it's the same object). So we should follow that pattern.

- `formatPipeClauseInIndentedStyle(first)`:
```
this.layout.add(this.showKw(node.nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();
this.layout.add(WS.INDENT);
this.layout = this.formatSubExpression(node.children);
this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();
```

- `formatNestedPipeClauseInIndentedStyle(clause)`:
```
this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();
this.layout.add(WS.INDENT);
this.layout = this.formatSubExpression(node.children);
this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();
```

Wait, for nested clause, we want it at level N+1. At the point after first clause's indented body, the indentation is back to N (after
decreaseTopLevel). So `add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT)` at level N would put GROUP BY at level N, not N+1. We need to increase before the keyword. Let me
re-trace:

After first indented pipe clause:
- We did increaseTopLevel -> N+1, formatted children, decreaseTopLevel -> N.
For nested GROUP BY, we want GROUP BY at N+1. So:
```
this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();  // N+1
this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);  // GROUP BY at N+1
this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();  // N+2
this.layout.add(WS.INDENT);
format children;
this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();  // N+1
this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();  // N
```

Hmm that increases twice. Alternatively, since after first clause we're at N, for nested:
```
increaseTopLevel();  // N+1
add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
increaseTopLevel();  // N+2
add(WS.INDENT);
format children;
decreaseTopLevel();  // N+1
decreaseTopLevel();  // N
```

That works but is a bit awkward. Actually, we could keep the first clause's indentation increased while formatting nested clauses. Let me restructure:
instead of decreasing after first clause, keep the level at N+1 while formatting rest clauses, then decrease at the end. But that only works if the
first clause is indented; if one-line, the "body" is inline and there's no N+1 context.

Let me think simpler. For the pipe node as a whole:
- We want the first clause keyword at level N (after pipeOp on same line).
- If indented: body at N+1.
- Subsequent clauses (GROUP BY) at N+1, their body at N+2.

So we can do:
```
formatPipe(node):
  add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(pipeOp), WS.SPACE);
  const [first, ...rest] = node.clauses;
  if (first is indented) {
    add(showKw(first.nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
    increaseTopLevel();  // N+1
    add(WS.INDENT);
    layout = formatSubExpression(first.children);  // body at N+1
    // now format rest clauses while staying at N+1
    for (clause of rest) {
      add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, showKw(clause.nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);  // at N+1
      increaseTopLevel();  // N+2
      add(WS.INDENT);
      layout = formatSubExpression(clause.children);  // body at N+2
      decreaseTopLevel();  // back to N+1
    }
    decreaseTopLevel();  // back to N
  } else {
    // one-line first
    add(showKw(first.nameKw), WS.SPACE);
    layout = formatSubExpression(first.children);
    // rest clauses (rare)
    for (clause of rest) {
      if (clause indented) { ... } else { ... }
    }
  }
```

This is clean. Let me handle rest clauses for one-line first too, though unlikely. For one-line nested clause, we'd do `add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT,
showKw(clause.nameKw), WS.SPACE)` at current level and format children inline. But if first is one-line at N, then rest clauses at N. Fine.

Hmm, but for SELECT we want to reuse select_clause's special handling (asterisk etc.). For LIMIT we want to reuse limit_clause's special handling? For

Actually we can create generic clause nodes for all pipe clauses, including SELECT and LIMIT, using free_form_sql. The special select_clause handling
for `SELECT *` is needed because `*` is ASTERISK token which is allowed in free_form_sql (asterisk is part of free_form_sql). Actually `free_form_sql`

That's simpler and gives one-line behavior. The requirement says LIMIT keeps content on same line, so generic is fine. `|> LIMIT 5, 10` -> children

```
pipe_clause -> %RESERVED_CLAUSE _ free_form_sql:* {%

```

But wait, if `_` is included between keyword and children, then children's leading comments would be captured separately. Actually `_` is optional

behavior, I'll use `%RESERVED_CLAUSE free_form_sql:*` without `_` between keyword and children. For comments between `|>` and keyword, `_` in pipe

import { isReserved, Token, TokenType } from './token.js';

/**
 * Ensures that no keyword token (RESERVED_*) is preceded or followed by a dot (.)
 * or any other property-access operator.
 *
 * Ensures that all RESERVED_FUNCTION_NAME tokens are followed by "(".
 * If they're not, converts the token to IDENTIFIER.
 *
 * Converts RESERVED_DATA_TYPE tokens followed by "(" to RESERVED_PARAMETERIZED_DATA_TYPE.
 *
 * When IDENTIFIER or RESERVED_DATA_TYPE token is followed by "["
 * converts it to ARRAY_IDENTIFIER or ARRAY_KEYWORD accordingly.
 *
 * This is needed to avoid ambiguity in parser which expects function names
 * to always be followed by open-paren, and to distinguish between
 * array accessor `foo[1]` and array literal `[1, 2, 3]`.
 */
export function disambiguateTokens(tokens: Token[]): Token[] {
  return tokens
    .map(propertyNameKeywordToIdent)
    .map(funcNameToIdent)
    .map(dataTypeToParameterizedDataType)
    .map(identToArrayIdent)
    .map(dataTypeToArrayKeyword);
}

const propertyNameKeywordToIdent = (token: Token, i: number, tokens: Token[]): Token => {
  if (isReserved(token.type)) {
    const prevToken = prevNonCommentToken(tokens, i);
    if (prevToken && prevToken.type === TokenType.PROPERTY_ACCESS_OPERATOR) {
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.IDENTIFIER, text: token.raw };
    }
    const nextToken = nextNonCommentToken(tokens, i);
    if (nextToken && nextToken.type === TokenType.PROPERTY_ACCESS_OPERATOR) {
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.IDENTIFIER, text: token.raw };
    }
  }
  return token;
};

const funcNameToIdent = (token: Token, i: number, tokens: Token[]): Token => {
  if (token.type === TokenType.RESERVED_FUNCTION_NAME) {
    const nextToken = nextNonCommentToken(tokens, i);
    if (!nextToken || !isOpenParen(nextToken)) {
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.IDENTIFIER, text: token.raw };
    }
  }
  return token;
};

const dataTypeToParameterizedDataType = (token: Token, i: number, tokens: Token[]): Token => {
  if (token.type === TokenType.RESERVED_DATA_TYPE) {
    const nextToken = nextNonCommentToken(tokens, i);
    if (nextToken && isOpenParen(nextToken)) {
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.RESERVED_PARAMETERIZED_DATA_TYPE };
    }
  }
  return token;
};

const identToArrayIdent = (token: Token, i: number, tokens: Token[]): Token => {
  if (token.type === TokenType.IDENTIFIER) {
    const nextToken = nextNonCommentToken(tokens, i);
    if (nextToken && isOpenBracket(nextToken)) {
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.ARRAY_IDENTIFIER };
    }
  }
  return token;
};

const dataTypeToArrayKeyword = (token: Token, i: number, tokens: Token[]): Token => {
  if (token.type === TokenType.RESERVED_DATA_TYPE) {
    const nextToken = nextNonCommentToken(tokens, i);
    if (nextToken && isOpenBracket(nextToken)) {
      return { ...token, type: TokenType.ARRAY_KEYWORD };
    }
  }
  return token;
};

const prevNonCommentToken = (tokens: Token[], index: number): Token | undefined =>
  nextNonCommentToken(tokens, index, -1);

const nextNonCommentToken = (
  tokens: Token[],
  index: number,
  dir: -1 | 1 = 1
): Token | undefined => {
  let i = 1;
  while (tokens[index + i * dir] && isComment(tokens[index + i * dir])) {
    i++;
  }
  return tokens[index + i * dir];
};

const isOpenParen = (t: Token): boolean => t.type === TokenType.OPEN_PAREN && t.text === '(';

const isOpenBracket = (t: Token): boolean => t.type === TokenType.OPEN_PAREN && t.text === '[';

const isComment = (t: Token): boolean =>
  t.type === TokenType.BLOCK_COMMENT || t.type === TokenType.LINE_COMMENT;

import { IndentStyle } from '../FormatOptions.js';
import { isLogicalOperator, TokenType } from '../lexer/token.js';

/**
 * When tabular style enabled,
 * produces a 10-char wide version of token text.
 */
export default function toTabularFormat(tokenText: string, indentStyle: IndentStyle): string {
  if (indentStyle === 'standard') {
    return tokenText;
  }

  let tail = [] as string[]; // rest of keyword
  if (tokenText.length >= 10 && tokenText.includes(' ')) {
    // split for long keywords like INNER JOIN or UNION DISTINCT
    [tokenText, ...tail] = tokenText.split(' ');
  }

  if (indentStyle === 'tabularLeft') {
    tokenText = tokenText.padEnd(9, ' ');
  } else {
    tokenText = tokenText.padStart(9, ' ');
  }

  return tokenText + ['', ...tail].join(' ');
}

/**
 * True when the token can be formatted in tabular style
 */
export function isTabularToken(type: TokenType): boolean {
  return (
    isLogicalOperator(type) ||
    type === TokenType.RESERVED_CLAUSE ||
    type === TokenType.RESERVED_SELECT ||
    type === TokenType.RESERVED_SET_OPERATION ||
    type === TokenType.RESERVED_JOIN ||
    type === TokenType.LIMIT
  );
}

// eslint-disable-next-line max-classes-per-file
import Indentation from './Indentation.js';
import Layout, { WS } from './Layout.js';

/**
 * Like Layout, but only formats single-line expressions.
 *
 * Throws InlineLayoutError:
 * - when encountering a newline
 * - when exceeding configured expressionWidth
 */
export default class InlineLayout extends Layout {
  private length = 0;
  // Keeps track of the trailing whitespace,
  // so that we can decrease length when encountering WS.NO_SPACE,
  // but only when there actually is a space to remove.
  private trailingSpace = false;

  constructor(private expressionWidth: number) {
    super(new Indentation('')); // no indentation in inline layout
  }

  public add(...items: (WS | string)[]) {
    items.forEach(item => this.addToLength(item));
    if (this.length > this.expressionWidth) {
      // We have exceeded the allowable width
      throw new InlineLayoutError();
    }
    super.add(...items);
  }

  private addToLength(item: WS | string) {
    if (typeof item === 'string') {
      this.length += item.length;
      this.trailingSpace = false;
    } else if (item === WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE || item === WS.NEWLINE) {
      // newlines not allowed within inline block
      throw new InlineLayoutError();
    } else if (item === WS.INDENT || item === WS.SINGLE_INDENT || item === WS.SPACE) {
      if (!this.trailingSpace) {
        this.length++;
        this.trailingSpace = true;
      }
    } else if (item === WS.NO_NEWLINE || item === WS.NO_SPACE) {
      if (this.trailingSpace) {
        this.trailingSpace = false;
        this.length--;
      }
    }
  }
}

/**
 * Thrown when block of SQL can't be formatted as a single line.
 */
export class InlineLayoutError extends Error {}

"Mixed pipe and traditional statements format independently." Since each statement is parsed/formatted separately, fine.

Now let's think about AGGREGATE promotion more. In `postProcess`, we need to promote AGGREGATE/EXTEND after PIPE. But the tokenizer's postProcess runs
before disambiguateTokens (in createParser: `disambiguateTokens(tokenizer.tokenize(...))`). So after promotion, disambiguateTokens will see AGGREGATE
as RESERVED_CLAUSE and not convert it (isReserved includes RESERVED_CLAUSE, but only converts if next to property access; so `AGGREGATE` with no dot
stays RESERVED_CLAUSE). Good.

But there's a subtlety: what if `AGGREGATE` is followed by `(` (function call)? E.g. `|> AGGREGATE SUM(x) ...` — AGGREGATE is followed by SUM, not
`(`. If someone writes `|> AGGREGATE (SELECT ...)`, then AGGREGATE is RESERVED_CLAUSE, no issue.

Now, what about `EXTEND` promotion: `|> EXTEND a + 1 AS b`. EXTEND promoted to RESERVED_CLAUSE, then pipe_clause matches. Good.

Now, another subtlety: The tokenizer rule order. `AGGREGATE` and `EXTEND` are not in any reserved list, so they're IDENTIFIER. After promotion, they
become RESERVED_CLAUSE. But `promotePipeClauseKeywords` runs in postProcess, which is applied after tokenize. Good.

import { TokenType } from '../lexer/token.js';

export enum NodeType {
  statement = 'statement',
  clause = 'clause',
  set_operation = 'set_operation',
  function_call = 'function_call',
  parameterized_data_type = 'parameterized_data_type',
  array_subscript = 'array_subscript',
  property_access = 'property_access',
  parenthesis = 'parenthesis',
  between_predicate = 'between_predicate',
  case_expression = 'case_expression',
  case_when = 'case_when',
  case_else = 'case_else',
  limit_clause = 'limit_clause',
  all_columns_asterisk = 'all_columns_asterisk',
  literal = 'literal',
  identifier = 'identifier',
  keyword = 'keyword',
  data_type = 'data_type',
  parameter = 'parameter',
  operator = 'operator',
  comma = 'comma',
  line_comment = 'line_comment',
  block_comment = 'block_comment',
  disable_comment = 'disable_comment',
}

interface BaseNode {
  leadingComments?: CommentNode[];
  trailingComments?: CommentNode[];
}

export interface StatementNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.statement;
  children: AstNode[];
  hasSemicolon: boolean;
}

export interface ClauseNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.clause;
  nameKw: KeywordNode;
  children: AstNode[];
}

export interface SetOperationNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.set_operation;
  nameKw: KeywordNode;
  children: AstNode[];
}

export interface FunctionCallNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.function_call;
  nameKw: KeywordNode;
  parenthesis: ParenthesisNode;
}

export interface ParameterizedDataTypeNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.parameterized_data_type;
  dataType: DataTypeNode;
  parenthesis: ParenthesisNode;
}

// <ident>[<expr>]
export interface ArraySubscriptNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.array_subscript;
  array: IdentifierNode | KeywordNode | DataTypeNode;
  parenthesis: ParenthesisNode;
}

export interface ParenthesisNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.parenthesis;
  children: AstNode[];
  openParen: string;
  closeParen: string;
}

// BETWEEN <expr1> AND <expr2>
export interface BetweenPredicateNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.between_predicate;
  betweenKw: KeywordNode;
  expr1: AstNode[];
  andKw: KeywordNode;
  expr2: AstNode[];
}

export interface CaseExpressionNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.case_expression;
  caseKw: KeywordNode;
  endKw: KeywordNode;
  expr: AstNode[];
  clauses: (CaseWhenNode | CaseElseNode)[];
}

export interface CaseWhenNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.case_when;
  whenKw: KeywordNode;
  thenKw: KeywordNode;
  condition: AstNode[];
  result: AstNode[];
}

export interface CaseElseNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.case_else;
  elseKw: KeywordNode;
  result: AstNode[];
}

// LIMIT <count>
// LIMIT <offset>, <count>
export interface LimitClauseNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.limit_clause;
  limitKw: KeywordNode;
  count: AstNode[];
  offset?: AstNode[];
}

// The "*" operator used in SELECT *
export interface AllColumnsAsteriskNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.all_columns_asterisk;
}

export interface LiteralNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.literal;
  text: string;
}

export interface PropertyAccessNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.property_access;
  object: AstNode;
  operator: string;
  property: IdentifierNode | ArraySubscriptNode | AllColumnsAsteriskNode;
}

export interface IdentifierNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.identifier;
  quoted: boolean;
  text: string;
}

export interface DataTypeNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.data_type;
  text: string;
  raw: string;
}

export interface KeywordNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.keyword;
  tokenType: TokenType;
  text: string;
  raw: string;
}

export interface ParameterNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.parameter;
  key?: string;
  text: string;
}

export interface OperatorNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.operator;
  text: string;
}

export interface CommaNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.comma;
}

export interface LineCommentNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.line_comment;
  text: string;
  precedingWhitespace: string;
}

export interface BlockCommentNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.block_comment;
  text: string;
  precedingWhitespace: string;
}

export interface DisableCommentNode extends BaseNode {
  type: NodeType.disable_comment;
  text: string;
  precedingWhitespace: string;
}

export type CommentNode = LineCommentNode | BlockCommentNode | DisableCommentNode;

  | ClauseNode

  | OperatorNode
  | CommaNode
  | LineCommentNode
  | BlockCommentNode
  | DisableCommentNode;

Add after other_clause / before set_operation:

```
    type: NodeType.pipe,

pipe -> %PIPE _ pipe_as_clause {%

    type: NodeType.pipe,
    pipeOp: addComments(toKeywordNode(pipeToken), { trailing: _ }),
    clauses: [clause],

  ( %RESERVED_CLAUSE
  | %RESERVED_JOIN

  ([nameToken, children]) => ({
    type: NodeType.clause,
    nameKw: toKeywordNode(nameToken),
    children,
  })
%}
pipe_clause -> select_clause {% unwrap %}

pipe_as_clause -> %RESERVED_KEYWORD free_form_sql:* {%
  ([nameToken, children]) => ({
    type: NodeType.clause,
    nameKw: toKeywordNode(nameToken),
    children,
  })
%}
```

Wait, there's a subtlety: the first `pipe` rule `%PIPE _ pipe_clause pipe_clause:*`. For input `|> SELECT a AS b`, pipe_clause matches select_clause
with children [a, AS, b], then pipe_clause:* matches zero. Good. For `|> AGGREGATE SUM(a) AS total GROUP BY b`, pipe_clause matches other_clause
AGGREGATE children [SUM(a), AS, total], pipe_clause:* matches [other_clause GROUP BY children [b]]. Good.

But there's a potential ambiguity: `pipe_clause` for RESERVED_CLAUSE uses `free_form_sql:*`. The `free_form_sql:*` can consume `AS` keyword, so
`AGGREGATE SUM(a) AS total` all consumed. Good. Then GROUP BY starts new pipe_clause.

But wait, what about `|> WHERE a > 1` where `a > 1` is free_form_sql. `>` is OPERATOR. `1` is literal. All free_form_sql. Good.

Now, what about `pipe_as_clause` matching when `pipe_clause` also could? For `|> AS foo`, pipe_clause's first alternative `%RESERVED_CLAUSE` doesn't
match AS (RESERVED_KEYWORD), `%RESERVED_JOIN` no, `%LIMIT` no, `select_clause` no. So only pipe_as_clause. Good.

But wait — what about `|> SET x = 1`? SET is RESERVED_CLAUSE (already in bigquery reservedClauses). So pipe_clause matches. Good.

What about `|> DROP x`? DROP is RESERVED_CLAUSE (in reservedClauses as part of DROP COLUMN etc.; DROP alone is RESERVED_CLAUSE? `reservedClauses`
includes `DROP [SNAPSHOT | EXTERNAL] TABLE [IF EXISTS]`, `DROP COLUMN [IF EXISTS]`, etc. — these are phrases; the word `DROP` alone might not be in
reservedClauses). Let me check: reservedClauses includes expandPhrases of `DROP ...` phrases. But `DROP` alone? Let's check the bigquery keywords
list: `DROP` is not in keywords. Hmm. If `DROP` alone isn't in reservedClauses, then `DROP` would be IDENTIFIER, not RESERVED_CLAUSE. Then `|> DROP b`
wouldn't parse as pipe_clause.

Wait, let me check `expandPhrases`. For `'DROP [SNAPSHOT | EXTERNAL] TABLE [IF EXISTS]'`, does it expand to include just 'DROP'? Let's look at
expandPhrases.

/**
 * Performs expandSinglePhrase() on array
 */
export const expandPhrases = (phrases: string[]): string[] => phrases.flatMap(expandSinglePhrase);

/**
 * Expands a syntax description like
 *
 *     "CREATE [OR REPLACE] [TEMP|TEMPORARY] TABLE"
 *
 * into an array of all possible combinations like:
 *
 *     [ "CREATE TABLE",
 *       "CREATE TEMP TABLE",
 *       "CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE",
 *       "CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE",
 *       "CREATE OR REPLACE TEMP TABLE",
 *       "CREATE OR REPLACE TEMPORARY TABLE" ]
 *
 * The [] and {} parenthesis can also be nested like
 *
 *     "FOR [OF {UNIQUE | MANDATORY} TABLES]"
 *
 * resulting in:
 *
 *     [ "FOR",
 *       "FOR OF UNIQUE TABLES",
 *       "FOR OF MANDATORY TABLES" ]
 */
export const expandSinglePhrase = (phrase: string): string[] =>
  buildCombinations(parsePhrase(phrase)).map(stripExtraWhitespace);

const stripExtraWhitespace = (text: string) => text.replace(/ +/g, ' ').trim();

const parsePhrase = (text: string): Phrase => ({
  type: 'mandatory_block',
  items: parseAlteration(text, 0)[0],
});

type Phrase = string | MandatoryBlock | OptionalBlock | Concatenation;
type Concatenation = { type: 'concatenation'; items: Phrase[] };
type MandatoryBlock = { type: 'mandatory_block'; items: Phrase[] };
type OptionalBlock = { type: 'optional_block'; items: Phrase[] };

const parseAlteration = (
  text: string,
  index: number,
  expectClosing?: ']' | '}'
): [Phrase[], number] => {
  const alterations: Phrase[] = [];
  while (text[index]) {
    const [term, newIndex] = parseConcatenation(text, index);
    alterations.push(term);
    index = newIndex;
    if (text[index] === '|') {
      index++;
    } else if (text[index] === '}' || text[index] === ']') {
      if (expectClosing !== text[index]) {
        throw new Error(`Unbalanced parenthesis in: ${text}`);
      }
      index++;
      return [alterations, index];
    } else if (index === text.length) {
      if (expectClosing) {
        throw new Error(`Unbalanced parenthesis in: ${text}`);
      }
      return [alterations, index];
    } else {
      throw new Error(`Unexpected "${text[index]}"`);
    }
  }
  return [alterations, index];
};

const parseConcatenation = (text: string, index: number): [Phrase, number] => {
  const items: Phrase[] = [];
  while (true) {
    const [term, newIndex] = parseTerm(text, index);
    if (term) {
      items.push(term);
      index = newIndex;
    } else {
      break;
    }
  }
  return items.length === 1 ? [items[0], index] : [{ type: 'concatenation', items }, index];
};

const parseTerm = (text: string, index: number): [Phrase, number] => {
  if (text[index] === '{') {
    return parseMandatoryBlock(text, index + 1);
  } else if (text[index] === '[') {
    return parseOptionalBlock(text, index + 1);
  } else {
    let word = '';
    while (text[index] && /[A-Za-z0-9_ ]/.test(text[index])) {
      word += text[index];
      index++;
    }
    return [word, index];
  }
};

const parseMandatoryBlock = (text: string, index: number): [MandatoryBlock, number] => {
  const [items, newIndex] = parseAlteration(text, index, '}');
  return [{ type: 'mandatory_block', items }, newIndex];
};

const parseOptionalBlock = (text: string, index: number): [OptionalBlock, number] => {
  const [items, newIndex] = parseAlteration(text, index, ']');
  return [{ type: 'optional_block', items }, newIndex];
};

const buildCombinations = (node: Phrase): string[] => {
  if (typeof node === 'string') {
    return [node];
  } else if (node.type === 'concatenation') {
    return node.items.map(buildCombinations).reduce(stringCombinations, ['']);
  } else if (node.type === 'mandatory_block') {
    return node.items.flatMap(buildCombinations);
  } else if (node.type === 'optional_block') {
    return ['', ...node.items.flatMap(buildCombinations)];
  } else {
    throw new Error(`Unknown node type: ${node}`);
  }
};

const stringCombinations = (xs: string[], ys: string[]): string[] => {
  const results: string[] = [];
  for (const x of xs) {
    for (const y of ys) {
      results.push(x + y);
    }
  }
  return results;
};

import { sortByLengthDesc } from '../utils.js';

import { IdentChars, QuoteType, VariableType } from './TokenizerOptions.js';
import { escapeRegExp, patternToRegex, prefixesPattern, withDashes } from './regexUtil.js';

/**
 * Builds a RegExp for valid line comments in a SQL dialect
 * @param {string[]} lineCommentTypes - list of character strings that denote line comments
 */
export const lineComment = (lineCommentTypes: string[]) =>
  new RegExp(`(?:${lineCommentTypes.map(escapeRegExp).join('|')}).*?(?=\r\n|\r|\n|$)`, 'uy');

/**
 * Builds a RegExp for matching either open- or close-parenthesis patterns
 */
export const parenthesis = (kind: 'open' | 'close', extraParens: ('[]' | '{}')[] = []): RegExp => {
  const index = kind === 'open' ? 0 : 1;
  const parens = ['()', ...extraParens].map(pair => pair[index]);
  return patternToRegex(parens.map(escapeRegExp).join('|'));
};

/**
 * Builds a RegExp containing all operators for a SQL dialect
 */
export const operator = (operators: string[]) =>
  patternToRegex(`${sortByLengthDesc(operators).map(escapeRegExp).join('|')}`);

// Negative lookahead to avoid matching a keyword that's actually part of identifier,
// which can happen when identifier allows word-boundary characters inside it.
//
// For example "SELECT$ME" should be tokenized as:
// - ["SELECT$ME"] when $ is allowed inside identifiers
// - ["SELECT", "$", "ME"] when $ can't be part of identifiers.
const rejectIdentCharsPattern = ({ rest, dashes }: IdentChars): string =>
  rest || dashes ? `(?![${rest || ''}${dashes ? '-' : ''}])` : '';

/**
 * Builds a RegExp for all Reserved Keywords in a SQL dialect
 */
export const reservedWord = (reservedKeywords: string[], identChars: IdentChars = {}): RegExp => {
  if (reservedKeywords.length === 0) {
    return /^\b$/u;
  }

  const avoidIdentChars = rejectIdentCharsPattern(identChars);

  const reservedKeywordsPattern = sortByLengthDesc(reservedKeywords)
    .map(escapeRegExp)
    .join('|')
    .replace(/ /gu, '\\s+');

  return new RegExp(`(?:${reservedKeywordsPattern})${avoidIdentChars}\\b`, 'iuy');
};

/**
 * Builds a RegExp for parameter placeholder patterns
 * @param {string[]} paramTypes - list of strings that denote placeholder types
 * @param {string} pattern - string that denotes placeholder pattern
 */
export const parameter = (paramTypes: string[], pattern: string): RegExp | undefined => {
  if (!paramTypes.length) {
    return undefined;
  }
  const typesRegex = paramTypes.map(escapeRegExp).join('|');

  return patternToRegex(`(?:${typesRegex})(?:${pattern})`);
};

const buildQStringPatterns = () => {
  const specialDelimiterMap = {
    '<': '>',
    '[': ']',
    '(': ')',
    '{': '}',
  };

  // base pattern for special delimiters, left must correspond with right
  const singlePattern = "{left}(?:(?!{right}').)*?{right}";

  // replace {left} and {right} with delimiters, collect as array
  const patternList = Object.entries(specialDelimiterMap).map(([left, right]) =>
    singlePattern.replace(/{left}/g, escapeRegExp(left)).replace(/{right}/g, escapeRegExp(right))
  );

  const specialDelimiters = escapeRegExp(Object.keys(specialDelimiterMap).join(''));
  // standard pattern for common delimiters, ignores special delimiters
  const standardDelimiterPattern = String.raw`(?<tag>[^\s${specialDelimiters}])(?:(?!\k<tag>').)*?\k<tag>`;

  // constructs final pattern by joining all cases
  const qStringPattern = `[Qq]'(?:${standardDelimiterPattern}|${patternList.join('|')})'`;

  return qStringPattern;
};

// Regex patterns for all supported quote styles.
//
// Most of them have a single escaping-style built in,
// but "" and '' support multiple versions of escapes,
// which must be selected with suffixes: -qq, -bs, -qq-bs, -raw
export const quotePatterns = {
  // - backtick quoted (using `` to escape)
  '``': '(?:`[^`]*`)+',
  // - Transact-SQL square bracket quoted (using ]] to escape)
  '[]': String.raw`(?:\[[^\]]*\])(?:\][^\]]*\])*`,
  // double-quoted
  '""-qq': String.raw`(?:"[^"]*")+`, // with repeated quote escapes
  '""-bs': String.raw`(?:"[^"\\]*(?:\\.[^"\\]*)*")`, // with backslash escapes
  '""-qq-bs': String.raw`(?:"[^"\\]*(?:\\.[^"\\]*)*")+`, // with repeated quote or backslash escapes
  '""-raw': String.raw`(?:"[^"]*")`, // no escaping
  // single-quoted
  "''-qq": String.raw`(?:'[^']*')+`, // with repeated quote escapes
  "''-bs": String.raw`(?:'[^'\\]*(?:\\.[^'\\]*)*')`, // with backslash escapes
  "''-qq-bs": String.raw`(?:'[^'\\]*(?:\\.[^'\\]*)*')+`, // with repeated quote or backslash escapes
  "''-raw": String.raw`(?:'[^']*')`, // no escaping
  // PostgreSQL dollar-quoted
  '$$': String.raw`(?<tag>\$\w*\$)[\s\S]*?\k<tag>`,
  // BigQuery '''triple-quoted''' (using \' to escape)
  "'''..'''": String.raw`'''[^\\]*?(?:\\.[^\\]*?)*?'''`,
  // BigQuery """triple-quoted""" (using \" to escape)
  '""".."""': String.raw`"""[^\\]*?(?:\\.[^\\]*?)*?"""`,

```ts
private formatPipe(node: PipeNode) {
  this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.pipeOp), WS.SPACE);

  const [first, ...rest] = node.clauses;
  this.formatPipeClause(first, true);

  for (const clause of rest) {
    this.formatPipeClause(clause, false);
  }
}

private formatPipeClause(node: ClauseNode, isFirst: boolean) {
  if (this.isOnelinePipeClause(node)) {
    this.layout.add(this.showKw(node.nameKw), WS.SPACE);
    this.layout = this.formatSubExpression(node.children);
  } else {
    if (isFirst) {
      this.layout.add(this.showKw(node.nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
      this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();
      this.layout.add(WS.INDENT);
      this.layout = this.formatSubExpression(node.children);
      this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();
    } else {
      this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();
      this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, this.showKw(node.nameKw), WS.NEWLINE);
      this.layout.indentation.increaseTopLevel();
      this.layout.add(WS.INDENT);
      this.layout = this.formatSubExpression(node.children);
      this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();
      this.layout.indentation.decreaseTopLevel();
    }
  }
}
```

  private splitBlockComment(comment: string): string[] {
    if (this.isDocComment(comment)) {
      return comment.split(/\n/).map(line => {
        if (/^\s*\*/.test(line)) {
          return ' ' + line.replace(/^\s*/, '');
        } else {
          return line;
        }
      });
    } else {
      return comment.split(/\n/).map(line => line.replace(/^\s*/, ''));
    }
  }

      this.layout.add(WS.NO_SPACE, ',', WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT);
    } else {
      this.layout.add(WS.NO_SPACE, ',', WS.SPACE);
    }
  }

  private withComments(node: AstNode, fn: () => void) {
    this.formatComments(node.leadingComments);
    fn();
    this.formatComments(node.trailingComments);
  }

  private formatComments(comments: CommentNode[] | undefined) {
    if (!comments) {
      return;
    }
    comments.forEach(com => {
      if (com.type === NodeType.line_comment) {
        this.formatLineComment(com);
      } else {
        this.formatBlockComment(com);
      }
    });
  }

  private formatLineComment(node: LineCommentNode) {
    if (isMultiline(node.precedingWhitespace || '')) {
      this.layout.add(WS.NEWLINE, WS.INDENT, node.text, WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE, WS.INDENT);
    } else if (this.layout.getLayoutItems().length > 0) {
      this.layout.add(WS.NO_NEWLINE, WS.SPACE, node.text, WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE, WS.INDENT);
    } else {
      // comment is the first item in code - no need to add preceding spaces
      this.layout.add(node.text, WS.MANDATORY_NEWLINE, WS.INDENT);
    }
  }

  private formatBlockComment(node: BlockCommentNode | DisableCommentNode) {

OTHER STEP FIELDS
